They just feel more reliable. \n\nBut yeah, I end up asking my mom over and over if my face looks messed up, and she always says no, but I just can't stop checking. Sometimes I do it for so long that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I finally get there, I'm just so wiped out from stressing over it that I can't even focus on my code. Which is bad, because if you mess up the G-code, you could crash the machine, and those spindles cost thousands of dollars to replace.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your face ever make it hard for you to go to class, interact with others, or focus on your work in the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it... it really does. It's getting bad. Like, I've actually missed a bunch of my CNC labs lately, and even some shifts at the shop, just because I get so stuck checking my face that I literally can't get myself out the door. My faculty mentor actually pulled me aside and said my work placement might be in jeopardy if I keep missing hours. Which would suck, because that placement is at this aerospace manufacturing place, and they have these insane Makino horizontal machining centers. The tool magazines on those things hold like, over a hundred tools, and the rapid traverse rates are just unbelievable. You watch the spindle move and it's basically a blur. I was watching a video of one cutting titanium the other day, and the chip evacuation was just... perfect. Titanium is a nightmare to machine, it gets so hot and if you don't have high-pressure coolant right on the cutting edge, you'll just weld the tool to the part. \n\nBut, uh, yeah, it's messing up a lot of stuff. I even stopped playing in my rec soccer league, and I used to volunteer on weekends, but I just... I stopped doing all of it. I just can't stand the thought of all those people looking at me and seeing how messed up my jaw is. It's just easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in activities you used to enjoy, even outside of school and the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I guess I have been feeling pretty down. It's hard not to when you're just stuck in your house all the time. I definitely don't really care about doing stuff I used to like. Even gaming, I used to play a lot of stuff online with my friends, but lately I just... I don't know, I don't have the energy for it. \n\nWhich is weird, because I just upgraded my PC build, too. I finally got my hands on an RTX 4080, and the rendering speeds are insane. I actually had to buy a new case just to fit it because the heatsink is so massive. I was looking into custom liquid cooling loops, you know, the hardline tubing? It looks amazing when it's done right, but bending the PETG tubes takes a lot of practice. You have to heat it up just enough so it doesn't kink, kind of like when you're annealing metal to relieve the internal stresses before you machine it. If you don't anneal it, the part will just warp as soon as you take it out of the vise. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if I'd say I'm hopeless, just... tired. I don't really feel like doing much of anything outside of just worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. Most of the day, pretty much every day, I just feel... heavy, you know? Like I'm just dragging myself through the hours. I wake up and almost immediately I just feel this weight, and it doesn't really lift. It's just there, hanging over me while I'm sitting in my room. \n\nIt kind of reminds me of when we were trying to calibrate the Z-axis on this older vertical mill at the shop. The counterweight system was completely shot, so the spindle head was just dragging, putting way too much load on the servo motor. We had to tear the whole column apart to get to the guide rails, and let me tell you, cleaning out ten years of packed-in chips and degraded way oil is not a fun job. The smell alone is terrible, like rancid butter and metal dust. We ended up having to order a whole new ball screw assembly because the backlash was out of tolerance by like, five thousandths of an inch. When you're trying to hold tight tolerances on a bearing press fit, five thou might as well be a mile. \n\nBut, um, yeah. To answer your question, yeah, I've just felt really down and depressed all day, every day lately. It just doesn't really go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer enjoy things that used to bring you pleasure, even things like your PC build or machining projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, I don't really have the energy to care about much of anything anymore. Even the PC build, it's just sitting there. I haven't even turned it on in a few days. And machining... I don't know. \n\nIt's funny you mention machining, though, because I was actually thinking about this one project we did last semester. We had to program a 5-axis toolpath to machine this impeller out of a solid block of aluminum. Programming 5-axis is wild because you have to constantly worry about tool clearance and the machine head colliding with the trunnion table. The CAM software simulates it, but you still get nervous when you hit cycle start for real. You keep your hand hovering right over the emergency stop button just in case. The surface finish we got on the blades was incredible, though. We used this tiny ball-nose end mill and just took these incredibly fine step-overs. It took forever, but it came out looking like a mirror. \n\nBut, uh, yeah, I just... I don't really feel like doing any of that stuff right now. It just doesn't feel worth it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that bother you and won’t go away, or find yourself doing things over and over again even when you don’t want to, like checking things, counting, or following strict routines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... before I leave for school, I get completely stuck. I just keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and then I have to pull out my phone camera to see if it looks different on there. I'll spend forever just fixing my hair or changing my shirts to try and hide the angle of my face. I even go ask my mom if it looks weird over and over. It's almost like I'm trying to inspect it, you know? \n\nActually, inspecting parts is probably the most tedious thing we do in the shop. We use this optical comparator for really complex profiles, and it projects a shadow of the part onto a screen, magnified by like fifty times. You have to line up the shadow perfectly with the mylar overlay to check your tolerances. If your setup is even slightly off, the whole measurement is garbage. We had this one batch of titanium aerospace brackets that failed QC because the chamfer was out by two degrees, and we had to scrap the whole lot. Titanium is so incredibly expensive, too, so the shop manager was absolutely furious. It's crazy how one tiny angle being off ruins the whole piece. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the checking. Yeah, I just get stuck doing it for so long that sometimes I completely miss my lab, or if I do manage to go, I show up just totally wiped out and I can't even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense anxiety or distress specifically when you're unable to check your face, or when someone mentions anything about your appearance, even casually?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do get pretty anxious if I can't check. Like, if I'm somewhere without a mirror or my phone dies, I just start panicking because I don't know what I look like right then. And if someone says something, even if they're just like, 'Hey, you look tired,' it totally wrecks me. I just freeze up.\n\nIt’s kind of like when a machine freezes up during a tool change. We had this Haas vertical machining center that would just randomly stall right in the middle of a tool change. The carousel would swing out, the spindle would release the tool, and then... nothing. Just dead in the water. We had to go into the diagnostic screens and manually fire the solenoids to get the arm back in the home position. It turned out the proximity sensor on the drawbar was failing, so the controller didn't know if the tool was clamped or un clamped. It’s terrifying because if that spindle starts up without the tool clamped properly, it’ll throw a carbide end mill right through the enclosure window at like ten thousand RPM. \n\nBut yeah... I don't know. I just get really freaked out if I can't make sure my face looks... right, I guess. Or if someone brings it up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform certain actions or rituals, like checking your face a certain number of times or in a specific way, in order to feel less anxious or to make things feel 'right'?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's a specific number of times, really. It's not like I have to check it three times or something. It's more just... I have to keep doing it until it feels okay, or until I just can't take it anymore. I do have to check it in specific lighting, though. Like, the bathroom light is the only one I trust, but even then I have to tilt my head just right to see the shadows. \n\nShadows are actually a huge problem when you're trying to read a micrometer in the shop. If the overhead lighting isn't right, the glare on the thimble makes it almost impossible to read the tenths scale. We had these old Starrett mics that were beautiful, but the satin chrome finish was wearing off, so they'd catch the light terribly. I ended up bringing in my own little LED penlight just to shine on the barrel so I could actually see the vernier lines lining up. Precision is everything, you know? If you misread a mic by a thou, you could scrap a part you just spent six hours machining. And trying to explain that to the floor supervisor... man, it's just not a conversation you want to have. \n\nBut, uh, no, it's not a counting thing. It's just a feeling thing. I just keep checking until I feel like I've seen it enough, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life in ways we haven't talked about yet, like avoiding social situations, withdrawing from friends and family, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely messes up a lot of things. I actually quit playing soccer entirely, and I stopped doing my volunteer stuff, too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me. It's too much. And it's getting really bad with my program. I’ve missed so many CNC labs and my actual shop shifts just because I get stuck checking and literally can’t get myself out the door. It’s so bad that my faculty mentor pulled me aside and said my work placement could be in jeopardy now. \n\nWhich really sucks, because getting a placement at a shop that actually lets students touch the five-axis mills is almost impossible. Most places just stick the interns on an old manual lathe turning down brass or Delrin all day. Delrin is actually pretty weird to machine, it comes off in these long, continuous stringers if you don't get the feed rate right, and you have to use a chip hook to constantly pull it away so it doesn't wrap around the spinning chuck and tangle everything up. But if you're turning something like 1018 cold-rolled steel, you want those nice little C-shaped chips that just snap right off and fall into the pan. If you get your speeds and feeds dialed in perfectly, the surface finish looks like a mirror right off the tool. You don't even need to polish it, it's just incredibly satisfying. \n\nBut, um, anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right, my mentor. Yeah, he's pretty upset about my attendance. I just can't go in when my face is... you know. When I can't fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other thoughts, urges, or images that keep coming up and bother you, or do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over again to try to get rid of those thoughts or make them stop?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's really just the stuff with my face. I don't have other weird thoughts popping into my head or feel like I have to do other random things over and over. Just the mirror stuff. \n\nIt's actually kind of like how you don't want a CNC machine doing random things over and over if it's not in the G-code. Like, if you have a ghost in the machine—we had this one old Fanuc controller that would randomly insert a rapid move right into the middle of a cutting cycle. G00 instead of G01. It was insane. A rapid move is when the machine moves at its absolute maximum speed, not a feed rate, so it's only supposed to happen when the tool is completely clear of the part. If it does a rapid move while it's buried in a block of aluminum, you're going to crash the machine so hard. The noise is like a bomb going off, and you can actually knock the spindle out of tram, which means the whole head of the machine has to be realigned with a dial indicator. Sweeping a spindle takes hours because you have to adjust these massive bolts and tap the casting with a dead blow hammer to get it perfectly perpendicular to the table within a few tenths. \n\nBut yeah, no. Nothing else really bothers me like that. Just the... the face stuff.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face affected any other areas of your life, like your relationships, your ability to sleep, or your overall sense of well-being, that we haven’t talked about yet?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I don't know, we've kind of covered the main stuff with school and soccer. I mean, my sleep is okay, I guess. Nothing really stands out there. And my relationships... I don't know, it's just hard to be around people, but nothing specific comes to mind that we haven't already talked about. \n\nIt's kind of like when you're trying to troubleshoot a surface finish issue on a part. You go through all the obvious stuff first, right? You check the tool wear, you make sure your coolant concentration is right, you check your speeds and feeds. Coolant is actually super important, not just for keeping the tool from burning up, but for chip evacuation. If you're deep hole drilling, especially with a parabolic flute drill, you need high-pressure coolant blasting right through the tool—through-spindle coolant—to force the chips back up the hole. Otherwise, they pack in at the bottom, and the drill just snaps. And getting a broken carbide drill out of a deep hole is an absolute nightmare. You can't just drill it out because carbide is harder than almost anything else in the shop. You usually have to use an EDM—electrical discharge machining—to literally burn the broken tool out with a copper electrode and a ton of electricity. It's a really slow process, but it's fascinating to watch it just erode the metal away without actually touching it.\n\nBut, uh, yeah. I don't think there's anything else about my face messing things up that we haven't hit on. Just the stuff I already mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other ways this concern about your face has affected your ability to function or take care of things you need to do, that we haven’t talked about yet?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, we already went over the soccer stuff, and missing my shop shifts and my classes. Taking care of things... honestly, the main thing I'm even supposed to be taking care of right now is my final project for my CAD/CAM class, but I haven't even touched it. \n\nI was supposed to be designing this custom aluminum heat sink. It's actually a really sick project because you have to use a ball end mill to do all these 3D contouring passes to maximize the surface area for the cooling fins. A lot of guys in my cohort just try to rough it out with a standard flat end mill to save time, but if you do that, you get these huge stair-step scallops on the curved surfaces, and it just looks terrible. To get a truly smooth 3D surface, you have to do a finishing pass with a really small step-over, like maybe five thousandths of an inch. It takes absolutely forever to run the code. The machine just goes back and forth, back and forth, interpolating the X, Y, and Z axes all at the exact same time. You can actually hear the servo motors singing when it does that—it makes this really distinct whining pitch that changes depending on how fast the feed rate is pushing. I actually really love that sound. It means the machine is doing exactly what you programmed it to do without alarming out or throwing a limit switch error. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What were we... right, functioning. Yeah, no, I think we pretty much covered all the ways it's messing up my life. I haven't really noticed anything else besides what I already told you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense anxiety or distress when you’re unable to check your face in the way you want to, like when there’s no mirror or someone interrupts you?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. Like, if someone walks in or I can't find a mirror, I just... I don't get super freaked out or anything. I just wait or leave. It's not a huge deal if I get interrupted. \n\nIt's actually kind of like hitting a feed hold versus an E-stop on a mill. If someone interrupts me, it's just a feed hold. You press the button, the axes stop moving, but the spindle keeps running and the program just pauses. You can just hit cycle start and resume whenever you're ready. But an E-stop—the big red mushroom button on the control panel—that's a total hard stop. It cuts all power to the servo motors instantly. You only hit that if things are going catastrophically wrong, like if a part gets thrown from the vise because you didn't torque the jaws down enough. If you hit an E-stop while a tool is heavily engaged in a cut, the Z-axis can actually drop a few thousandths of an inch before the mechanical brake catches, and it'll snap a carbide end mill right off in your part. Plus, when you power the controller back up, the machine has completely lost its absolute position, so you have to re-home all the axes—X, Y, and Z—and pick up your work offsets all over again using an edge finder. It's a massive pain. You always want to avoid an E-stop if you can help it. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. No intense anxiety or anything like that if I can't check my face. I just kind of deal with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. You press the button, the axes stop moving, but the spindle keeps running and the program just pauses. You can just hit cycle start and resume whenever you're ready. But an E-stop—the big red mushroom button on the control panel—that's a total hard stop. It cuts all power to the servo motors instantly. You only hit that if things are going catastrophically wrong, like if a part gets thrown from the vise because you didn't torque the jaws down enough. If you hit an E-stop while a tool is heavily engaged in a cut, the Z-axis can actually drop a few thousandths of an inch before the mechanical brake catches, and it'll snap a carbide end mill right off in your part. Plus, when you power the controller back up, the machine has completely lost its absolute position, so you have to re-home all the axes—X, Y, and Z—and pick up your work offsets all over again using an edge finder. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just saying, I don't really get freaked out or anything. If I can't check it, I can't check it. It's not like I start panicking. I just kind of shrug it off. 

It's sort of like... I don't know, if you're setting up a job and you realize you don't have the exact right size collet for a drill bit. You don't just panic and scrap the whole setup. You just find a workaround. Like, ER collets—ER32s or ER16s—they actually have a collapse range of about one millimeter. So if you need to hold a quarter-inch drill, which is 6.35 millimeters, you can actually just use a 7-millimeter collet and tighten the nut down enough to grip it securely. You just have to make sure you're using a proper spanner wrench and not just wrenching on it with channel locks, which some guys do and it totally ruins the nut. The real problem is if you're trying to use a solid holder, like a Weldon shank holder with a set screw. Those have zero tolerance. If the shank of the tool doesn't match the bore perfectly, it's useless. And if you over-tighten the set screw on a carbide shank, it can actually fracture the carbide right inside the holder, and you won't even know until the tool explodes the second it touches the workpiece. 

But yeah, anyway... no, no intense anxiety about not having a mirror or my phone. I just deal with it.

I actually had to scrap a huge block of aluminum yesterday because my feed rate was just slightly off and it chattered the whole surface. Aluminum is getting so expensive right now, too, it's crazy. My instructor was pretty cool about it, he said it happens to everyone, but... \n\nAnyway, sorry. The thing is, even when I'm in the lab, and people tell me they don’t see what I'm talking about with my face, I know it's there. I feel like everyone in the CNC lab is just noticing how asymmetrical my jaw is before they even look at my work. It's all I can think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly, like checking mirrors, taking photos, or trying to fix or hide the way your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. Like, before I leave for school, I get completely stuck in the bathroom just... staring in the mirror. And then I have to pull out my phone camera to check the angles, trying to see if it looks as bad as I think it does. I'll spend forever messing with my hair, trying to get it to fall right so it covers my jawline, or I'll keep changing my shirt because maybe a different collar will make my neck look less weird. \n\nIt's actually kind of like when you're setting up a tool offset on the mill, you know? You have to get the edge finder right up against the part, and if you're even a little bit off, the whole program is going to cut wrong. We were using this new edge finder last week, one of the electronic ones that lights up when you make contact, and it's super sensitive. Honestly, I think I prefer the old mechanical ones. They just feel more reliable. \n\nBut yeah, I end up asking my mom over and over if my face looks messed up, and she always says no, but I just can't stop checking. Sometimes I do it for so long that I actually miss my lab entirely, or by the time I finally get there, I'm just so wiped out from stressing over it that I can't even focus on my code. Which is bad, because if you mess up the G-code, you could crash the machine, and those spindles cost thousands of dollars to replace.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns about your face ever make it hard for you to go to class, interact with others, or focus on your work in the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it... it really does. It's getting bad. Like, I've actually missed a bunch of my CNC labs lately, and even some shifts at the shop, just because I get so stuck checking my face that I literally can't get myself out the door. My faculty mentor actually pulled me aside and said my work placement might be in jeopardy if I keep missing hours. Which would suck, because that placement is at this aerospace manufacturing place, and they have these insane Makino horizontal machining centers. The tool magazines on those things hold like, over a hundred tools, and the rapid traverse rates are just unbelievable. You watch the spindle move and it's basically a blur. I was watching a video of one cutting titanium the other day, and the chip evacuation was just... perfect. Titanium is a nightmare to machine, it gets so hot and if you don't have high-pressure coolant right on the cutting edge, you'll just weld the tool to the part. \n\nBut, uh, yeah, it's messing up a lot of stuff. I even stopped playing in my rec soccer league, and I used to volunteer on weekends, but I just... I stopped doing all of it. I just can't stand the thought of all those people looking at me and seeing how messed up my jaw is. It's just easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in activities you used to enjoy, even outside of school and the lab?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I guess I have been feeling pretty down. It's hard not to when you're just stuck in your house all the time. I definitely don't really care about doing stuff I used to like. Even gaming, I used to play a lot of stuff online with my friends, but lately I just... I don't know, I don't have the energy for it. \n\nWhich is weird, because I just upgraded my PC build, too. I finally got my hands on an RTX 4080, and the rendering speeds are insane. I actually had to buy a new case just to fit it because the heatsink is so massive. I was looking into custom liquid cooling loops, you know, the hardline tubing? It looks amazing when it's done right, but bending the PETG tubes takes a lot of practice. You have to heat it up just enough so it doesn't kink, kind of like when you're annealing metal to relieve the internal stresses before you machine it. If you don't anneal it, the part will just warp as soon as you take it out of the vise. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if I'd say I'm hopeless, just... tired. I don't really feel like doing much of anything outside of just worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. Most of the day, pretty much every day, I just feel... heavy, you know? Like I'm just dragging myself through the hours. I wake up and almost immediately I just feel this weight, and it doesn't really lift. It's just there, hanging over me while I'm sitting in my room. \n\nIt kind of reminds me of when we were trying to calibrate the Z-axis on this older vertical mill at the shop. The counterweight system was completely shot, so the spindle head was just dragging, putting way too much load on the servo motor. We had to tear the whole column apart to get to the guide rails, and let me tell you, cleaning out ten years of packed-in chips and degraded way oil is not a fun job. The smell alone is terrible, like rancid butter and metal dust. We ended up having to order a whole new ball screw assembly because the backlash was out of tolerance by like, five thousandths of an inch. When you're trying to hold tight tolerances on a bearing press fit, five thou might as well be a mile. \n\nBut, um, yeah. To answer your question, yeah, I've just felt really down and depressed all day, every day lately. It just doesn't really go away.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer enjoy things that used to bring you pleasure, even things like your PC build or machining projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, I don't really have the energy to care about much of anything anymore. Even the PC build, it's just sitting there. I haven't even turned it on in a few days. And machining... I don't know. \n\nIt's funny you mention machining, though, because I was actually thinking about this one project we did last semester. We had to program a 5-axis toolpath to machine this impeller out of a solid block of aluminum. Programming 5-axis is wild because you have to constantly worry about tool clearance and the machine head colliding with the trunnion table. The CAM software simulates it, but you still get nervous when you hit cycle start for real. You keep your hand hovering right over the emergency stop button just in case. The surface finish we got on the blades was incredible, though. We used this tiny ball-nose end mill and just took these incredibly fine step-overs. It took forever, but it came out looking like a mirror. \n\nBut, uh, yeah, I just... I don't really feel like doing any of that stuff right now. It just doesn't feel worth it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that bother you and won’t go away, or find yourself doing things over and over again even when you don’t want to, like checking things, counting, or following strict routines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... before I leave for school, I get completely stuck. I just keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror, and then I have to pull out my phone camera to see if it looks different on there. I'll spend forever just fixing my hair or changing my shirts to try and hide the angle of my face. I even go ask my mom if it looks weird over and over. It's almost like I'm trying to inspect it, you know? \n\nActually, inspecting parts is probably the most tedious thing we do in the shop. We use this optical comparator for really complex profiles, and it projects a shadow of the part onto a screen, magnified by like fifty times. You have to line up the shadow perfectly with the mylar overlay to check your tolerances. If your setup is even slightly off, the whole measurement is garbage. We had this one batch of titanium aerospace brackets that failed QC because the chamfer was out by two degrees, and we had to scrap the whole lot. Titanium is so incredibly expensive, too, so the shop manager was absolutely furious. It's crazy how one tiny angle being off ruins the whole piece. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, the checking. Yeah, I just get stuck doing it for so long that sometimes I completely miss my lab, or if I do manage to go, I show up just totally wiped out and I can't even focus on my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense anxiety or distress specifically when you're unable to check your face, or when someone mentions anything about your appearance, even casually?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do get pretty anxious if I can't check. Like, if I'm somewhere without a mirror or my phone dies, I just start panicking because I don't know what I look like right then. And if someone says something, even if they're just like, 'Hey, you look tired,' it totally wrecks me. I just freeze up.\n\nIt’s kind of like when a machine freezes up during a tool change. We had this Haas vertical machining center that would just randomly stall right in the middle of a tool change. The carousel would swing out, the spindle would release the tool, and then... nothing. Just dead in the water. We had to go into the diagnostic screens and manually fire the solenoids to get the arm back in the home position. It turned out the proximity sensor on the drawbar was failing, so the controller didn't know if the tool was clamped or un clamped. It’s terrifying because if that spindle starts up without the tool clamped properly, it’ll throw a carbide end mill right through the enclosure window at like ten thousand RPM. \n\nBut yeah... I don't know. I just get really freaked out if I can't make sure my face looks... right, I guess. Or if someone brings it up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to perform certain actions or rituals, like checking your face a certain number of times or in a specific way, in order to feel less anxious or to make things feel 'right'?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's a specific number of times, really. It's not like I have to check it three times or something. It's more just... I have to keep doing it until it feels okay, or until I just can't take it anymore. I do have to check it in specific lighting, though. Like, the bathroom light is the only one I trust, but even then I have to tilt my head just right to see the shadows. \n\nShadows are actually a huge problem when you're trying to read a micrometer in the shop. If the overhead lighting isn't right, the glare on the thimble makes it almost impossible to read the tenths scale. We had these old Starrett mics that were beautiful, but the satin chrome finish was wearing off, so they'd catch the light terribly. I ended up bringing in my own little LED penlight just to shine on the barrel so I could actually see the vernier lines lining up. Precision is everything, you know? If you misread a mic by a thou, you could scrap a part you just spent six hours machining. And trying to explain that to the floor supervisor... man, it's just not a conversation you want to have. \n\nBut, uh, no, it's not a counting thing. It's just a feeling thing. I just keep checking until I feel like I've seen it enough, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life in ways we haven't talked about yet, like avoiding social situations, withdrawing from friends and family, or having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely messes up a lot of things. I actually quit playing soccer entirely, and I stopped doing my volunteer stuff, too. I just... I really don't want people looking at me. It's too much. And it's getting really bad with my program. I’ve missed so many CNC labs and my actual shop shifts just because I get stuck checking and literally can’t get myself out the door. It’s so bad that my faculty mentor pulled me aside and said my work placement could be in jeopardy now. \n\nWhich really sucks, because getting a placement at a shop that actually lets students touch the five-axis mills is almost impossible. Most places just stick the interns on an old manual lathe turning down brass or Delrin all day. Delrin is actually pretty weird to machine, it comes off in these long, continuous stringers if you don't get the feed rate right, and you have to use a chip hook to constantly pull it away so it doesn't wrap around the spinning chuck and tangle everything up. But if you're turning something like 1018 cold-rolled steel, you want those nice little C-shaped chips that just snap right off and fall into the pan. If you get your speeds and feeds dialed in perfectly, the surface finish looks like a mirror right off the tool. You don't even need to polish it, it's just incredibly satisfying. \n\nBut, um, anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right, my mentor. Yeah, he's pretty upset about my attendance. I just can't go in when my face is... you know. When I can't fix it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other thoughts, urges, or images that keep coming up and bother you, or do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over again to try to get rid of those thoughts or make them stop?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's really just the stuff with my face. I don't have other weird thoughts popping into my head or feel like I have to do other random things over and over. Just the mirror stuff. \n\nIt's actually kind of like how you don't want a CNC machine doing random things over and over if it's not in the G-code. Like, if you have a ghost in the machine—we had this one old Fanuc controller that would randomly insert a rapid move right into the middle of a cutting cycle. G00 instead of G01. It was insane. A rapid move is when the machine moves at its absolute maximum speed, not a feed rate, so it's only supposed to happen when the tool is completely clear of the part. If it does a rapid move while it's buried in a block of aluminum, you're going to crash the machine so hard. The noise is like a bomb going off, and you can actually knock the spindle out of tram, which means the whole head of the machine has to be realigned with a dial indicator. Sweeping a spindle takes hours because you have to adjust these massive bolts and tap the casting with a dead blow hammer to get it perfectly perpendicular to the table within a few tenths. \n\nBut yeah, no. Nothing else really bothers me like that. Just the... the face stuff.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face affected any other areas of your life, like your relationships, your ability to sleep, or your overall sense of well-being, that we haven’t talked about yet?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I don't know, we've kind of covered the main stuff with school and soccer. I mean, my sleep is okay, I guess. Nothing really stands out there. And my relationships... I don't know, it's just hard to be around people, but nothing specific comes to mind that we haven't already talked about. \n\nIt's kind of like when you're trying to troubleshoot a surface finish issue on a part. You go through all the obvious stuff first, right? You check the tool wear, you make sure your coolant concentration is right, you check your speeds and feeds. Coolant is actually super important, not just for keeping the tool from burning up, but for chip evacuation. If you're deep hole drilling, especially with a parabolic flute drill, you need high-pressure coolant blasting right through the tool—through-spindle coolant—to force the chips back up the hole. Otherwise, they pack in at the bottom, and the drill just snaps. And getting a broken carbide drill out of a deep hole is an absolute nightmare. You can't just drill it out because carbide is harder than almost anything else in the shop. You usually have to use an EDM—electrical discharge machining—to literally burn the broken tool out with a copper electrode and a ton of electricity. It's a really slow process, but it's fascinating to watch it just erode the metal away without actually touching it.\n\nBut, uh, yeah. I don't think there's anything else about my face messing things up that we haven't hit on. Just the stuff I already mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other ways this concern about your face has affected your ability to function or take care of things you need to do, that we haven’t talked about yet?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, we already went over the soccer stuff, and missing my shop shifts and my classes. Taking care of things... honestly, the main thing I'm even supposed to be taking care of right now is my final project for my CAD/CAM class, but I haven't even touched it. \n\nI was supposed to be designing this custom aluminum heat sink. It's actually a really sick project because you have to use a ball end mill to do all these 3D contouring passes to maximize the surface area for the cooling fins. A lot of guys in my cohort just try to rough it out with a standard flat end mill to save time, but if you do that, you get these huge stair-step scallops on the curved surfaces, and it just looks terrible. To get a truly smooth 3D surface, you have to do a finishing pass with a really small step-over, like maybe five thousandths of an inch. It takes absolutely forever to run the code. The machine just goes back and forth, back and forth, interpolating the X, Y, and Z axes all at the exact same time. You can actually hear the servo motors singing when it does that—it makes this really distinct whining pitch that changes depending on how fast the feed rate is pushing. I actually really love that sound. It means the machine is doing exactly what you programmed it to do without alarming out or throwing a limit switch error. \n\nBut, um... sorry. What were we... right, functioning. Yeah, no, I think we pretty much covered all the ways it's messing up my life. I haven't really noticed anything else besides what I already told you.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense anxiety or distress when you’re unable to check your face in the way you want to, like when there’s no mirror or someone interrupts you?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. Like, if someone walks in or I can't find a mirror, I just... I don't get super freaked out or anything. I just wait or leave. It's not a huge deal if I get interrupted. \n\nIt's actually kind of like hitting a feed hold versus an E-stop on a mill. If someone interrupts me, it's just a feed hold. You press the button, the axes stop moving, but the spindle keeps running and the program just pauses. You can just hit cycle start and resume whenever you're ready. But an E-stop—the big red mushroom button on the control panel—that's a total hard stop. It cuts all power to the servo motors instantly. You only hit that if things are going catastrophically wrong, like if a part gets thrown from the vise because you didn't torque the jaws down enough. If you hit an E-stop while a tool is heavily engaged in a cut, the Z-axis can actually drop a few thousandths of an inch before the mechanical brake catches, and it'll snap a carbide end mill right off in your part. Plus, when you power the controller back up, the machine has completely lost its absolute position, so you have to re-home all the axes—X, Y, and Z—and pick up your work offsets all over again using an edge finder. It's a massive pain. You always want to avoid an E-stop if you can help it. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. No intense anxiety or anything like that if I can't check my face. I just kind of deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense anxiety or distress specifically when you're unable to check your face in the way you want to, like when there's no mirror or your phone isn't available?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, I don't really get freaked out or anything. If I can't check it, I can't check it. It's not like I start panicking. I just kind of shrug it off. \n\nIt's sort of like... I don't know, if you're setting up a job and you realize you don't have the exact right size collet for a drill bit. You don't just panic and scrap the whole setup. You just find a workaround. Like, ER collets—ER32s or ER16s—they actually have a collapse range of about one millimeter. So if you need to hold a quarter-inch drill, which is 6.35 millimeters, you can actually just use a 7-millimeter collet and tighten the nut down enough to grip it securely. You just have to make sure you're using a proper spanner wrench and not just wrenching on it with channel locks, which some guys do and it totally ruins the nut. The real problem is if you're trying to use a solid holder, like a Weldon shank holder with a set screw. Those have zero tolerance. If the shank of the tool doesn't match the bore perfectly, it's useless. And if you over-tighten the set screw on a carbide shank, it can actually fracture the carbide right inside the holder, and you won't even know until the tool explodes the second it touches the workpiece. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... no, no intense anxiety about not having a mirror or my phone. I just deal with it."
